AI coding agents are adding a new layer to the software supply chain. Learn what Snyk found in nearly 10,000 developer environments and how to secure the tools, instructions, and permissions behind agentic development.

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — What nearly 10,000 developer environments reveal about agentic development risk
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

| order by Timestamp desc

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

When a vendor's breach becomes yours: lessons from the Klue incident

Snyk
2026-06-23 6 use cases 3 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

A forgotten credential at vendor Klue let attackers reach customers' Salesforce data. How modern SaaS breaches cascade, and the keys you should audit.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Salesforce connected-app OAuth access from first-seen ISP / anonymizing proxy (stolen-token reuse) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Klue/Icarus: bulk Salesforce CRM record retrieval via connected app (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) · [LLM] Salesforce API access bearing python-requests/aiohttp automation user-agent (Icarus OAuth abuse) · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Klue/Icarus: bulk Salesforce CRM record retrieval via connected app (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the high-volume automated read/export of Salesforce CRM objects performed by the compromised Klue OAuth integration during the June 2026 Icarus/UNC6395 campaign. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact CRM objects (Case, Contact, Account, Opportunity) that were mass-queried via SOQL/bulk export by an authorized third-party connected app rather than an interactive user. Rationale: Anchors on the exact CRM objects (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) that Mandiant/Google Cloud confirmed were bulk-SOQL-queried and exported by the abused OAuth connected app, plus a per-hour volume threshold that separates a productized SaaS connector's steady sync from an attacker's automated mass extraction. Cross-checked against: • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4187907/klue-breach-exposed-salesforce-crm-data-through-stolen-oauth-tokens.html • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift • https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoftthreatprotectionblog/protect-against-oauth-attacks-in-salesforce-with-microsoft-defender/4450584
ATT&CKT1119 T1213 T1567.002
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Nightly/scheduled Klue competitive-intel sync legitimately reading Account/Opportunity records
  • Sales-ops bulk report exports or Salesforce Data Loader jobs run by admins
  • Data-warehouse ETL connectors (Fivetran, Informatica, Mulesoft) extracting CRM objects on a schedule
  • SaaS backup tooling (OwnBackup/Own, Spanning) performing full-object snapshots
  • Marketing/RevOps enrichment integrations doing periodic full-table pulls
Suppression filter
| where AccountObjectId !in ("<known-klue-integration-objectid-AFTER-revalidation>") and not (UserAgents has_any ("Salesforce Data Loader","OwnBackup","Fivetran","Informatica"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the AccountObjectId/CONNECTED_APP_NAME driving the spike — confirm whether it is the Klue integration service account and compare the hour's RecordOps to the prior 30-day baseline for that identity. Pull the UserAgents and SrcIPs aggregated. 2) CONTAIN: If it is Klue (or any unexpected connected app) exceeding baseline, disable/revoke the connected app's OAuth authorization in Salesforce Setup > Connected Apps OAuth Usage and revoke its tokens; do not just block the IP. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the same AccountObjectId across all Salesforce ActionTypes for the last 14 days, look for python-requests/aiohttp user-agents (UC2) and first-seen source ISPs (UC3), and enumerate exactly which records were read for breach-notification scoping.
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where ActionType has_any ("Api","ReportExport","Report exported","Bulk","Mass","Export","Read")
| where ObjectType in~ ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity") or isempty(ObjectType)
| summarize RecordOps = count(),
            DistinctRecords = dcount(ObjectId),
            ObjectsTouched = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 10),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, ApplicationId, bin(Timestamp, 1h)
| where RecordOps > 1000   // bulk automated pull; productized Klue sync runs far lower, attacker mass-export spikes
| order by RecordOps desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
CloudAppEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where ActionType has_any ("Api","ReportExport","Report exported","Bulk","Mass","Export","Read")
| where ObjectType in~ ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity") or isempty(ObjectType)
| summarize RecordOps = count(),
            DistinctRecords = dcount(ObjectId),
            ObjectsTouched = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 10),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, ApplicationId, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| where RecordOps > 1000   // bulk automated pull; productized Klue sync runs far lower, attacker mass-export spikes
| order by RecordOps desc
WEB
sourcetype="sfdc:reportexport" OR sourcetype="sfdc:api" OR sourcetype="sfdc:restapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:bulkapi"
| eval crm_object=coalesce(ENTITY_NAME, ENTITY)
| where (crm_object IN ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity")) OR (EVENT_TYPE IN ("ReportExport","BulkApi","BulkApiV2"))
| bin _time span=1h
| stats count AS RecordOps dc(crm_object) AS DistinctObjects values(crm_object) AS Objects values(USER_AGENT) AS UserAgents dc(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPCount values(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPs by _time, USER_NAME, USER_ID, CONNECTED_APP_NAME
| where RecordOps > 1000
| sort - RecordOps
Salesforce API access bearing python-requests/aiohttp automation user-agent (Icarus OAuth abuse) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects Salesforce API/bulk calls whose user-agent is a raw Python HTTP library (python-requests, aiohttp), which Mandiant attributed to the Icarus/UNC6395 exfiltration scripts in the Klue/Salesloft-Drift OAuth campaign. Article-specific because a productized SaaS connector like Klue does not present python-requests/aiohttp as its session user-agent — that fingerprint indicates the stolen token is being driven by an attacker's ad-hoc script. Rationale: The python-requests/aiohttp user-agent is a high-fidelity, second-source-confirmed (Mandiant/Google Cloud) fingerprint of the Icarus exfil scripts driving a stolen OAuth token, and it cleanly distinguishes scripted attacker access from a legitimate productized connector's normal session UA. Cross-checked against: • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift • https://www.rescana.com/post/salesloft-drift-oauth-token-breach-enables-salesforce-data-theft-in-unc6395-icarus-attack-campaign-august-2026
ATT&CKT1528 T1078.004 T1071.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • In-house Python integration scripts legitimately calling the Salesforce REST/Bulk API with a default user-agent
  • CI/CD jobs or scheduled data pipelines built on requests/aiohttp pulling CRM data
  • QA/sandbox automation against a production org
  • A developer running an ad-hoc simple_salesforce/requests script for a one-off pull
  • Low-code/iPaaS connector that happens to surface a python-requests UA
Suppression filter
| where AccountObjectId !in ("<known-internal-python-integration-objectid>") and AccountType != "DeveloperSandbox"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Determine which AccountObjectId/CONNECTED_APP the python-requests/aiohttp sessions authenticated as — if it is an OAuth connected-app/service identity (not a named developer), treat as high priority and pull the SrcIPs and Objects accessed. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the connected app's OAuth tokens and disable the integration in Salesforce; rotate the integration credential/secret. 3) HUNT: Correlate the same SrcIPs/AccountObjectId against UC1 (bulk record volume) and UC3 (new source infra); if confirmed, enumerate every object/record touched by those sessions for notification scope and check for log-tampering (Setup Audit Trail gaps).
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where UserAgent has_any ("python-requests","aiohttp","python-urllib","Python/3")
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 12),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 12),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8),
            AnonProxy = make_set(IsAnonymousProxy, 3)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, UserAgent
| order by EventCount desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
CloudAppEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where UserAgent has_any ("python-requests","aiohttp","python-urllib","Python/3")
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 12),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 12),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),

            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, UserAgent

WEB
sourcetype="sfdc:api" OR sourcetype="sfdc:restapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:bulkapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:reportexport"
| where like(lower(USER_AGENT),"%python-requests%") OR like(lower(USER_AGENT),"%aiohttp%") OR like(lower(USER_AGENT),"%python-urllib%")
| stats count AS Hits values(EVENT_TYPE) AS EventTypes values(ENTITY_NAME) AS Objects dc(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPCount values(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPs min(_time) AS firstSeen max(_time) AS lastSeen by USER_NAME, USER_ID, USER_AGENT, CONNECTED_APP_NAME
| sort - Hits
Salesforce connected-app OAuth access from first-seen ISP / anonymizing proxy (stolen-token reuse) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces Salesforce activity for a connected-app/service identity arriving from a source ISP never previously associated with that identity, or flagged as an anonymizing proxy — the signature of an attacker replaying a harvested OAuth token from their own infrastructure rather than from Klue's. Article-specific because the breach hinged on a long-lived integration token being used from attacker-controlled (proxied) infrastructure. Rationale: Replays the core breach mechanic — a long-lived OAuth/service token used from new (often anonymized) infrastructure — by leftanti-joining today's Salesforce source ISPs per identity against a 30-day baseline and prioritizing anonymous-proxy hits, which catches token theft even when API calls themselves look legitimate. Cross-checked against: • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4187907/klue-breach-exposed-salesforce-crm-data-through-stolen-oauth-tokens.html • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift
ATT&CKT1078.004 T1528 T1090.003
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Klue (or another connected app) legitimately changing its egress IP/ISP after a cloud-provider migration or scaling event
  • A traveling admin or service account routed through a new corporate VPN/egress point
  • Genuine new integration deployed in the last 24h presenting a never-before-seen ISP
  • Cloud-hosted ETL/backup tooling whose provider rotates outbound IP ranges
  • Identity using a privacy-VPN endpoint flagged as anonymous proxy but operated by an employee
Suppression filter
| where not (ISP in ("<known-klue-egress-isp>","<corp-vpn-isp>") and AnyAnonProxy == false)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For each first-seen ISP/identity pair, confirm whether the AccountObjectId is a connected-app/service identity and whether the new ISP/anon-proxy is expected (check change tickets for new integrations or vendor IP changes). 2) CONTAIN: If unexplained — especially anonymous-proxy hits on the Klue integration identity — revoke the connected app's OAuth tokens, force token re-issuance, and rotate the integration secret. 3) HUNT: Join this identity+IP back to UC1 (bulk reads) and UC2 (python UAs) within the same window; if any correlate, scope all records accessed from those sessions and review the Setup Audit Trail for evidence of log clearing.
let Lookback = 30d;
let Baseline = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp between (ago(Lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where Application == "Salesforce"
    | summarize by AccountObjectId, ISP;
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where isnotempty(AccountObjectId)
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on AccountObjectId, ISP
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 10),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 8),
            AnyAnonProxy = max(tobool(IsAnonymousProxy))
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, IPAddress, ISP, CountryCode
| order by AnyAnonProxy desc, FirstSeen desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
let Lookback = 30d;
let Baseline = CloudAppEvents
    | where TimeGenerated between (ago(Lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where Application == "Salesforce"
    | summarize by AccountObjectId, ISP;
CloudAppEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where isnotempty(AccountObjectId)
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on AccountObjectId, ISP
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 10),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 8),
            AnyAnonProxy = max(tobool(IsAnonymousProxy))
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, IPAddress, ISP, CountryCode
| order by AnyAnonProxy desc, FirstSeen desc
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) AS firstSeen max(_time) AS lastSeen from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="Salesforce" earliest=-1d by Authentication.user, Authentication.src, Authentication.app
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| search NOT
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="Salesforce" earliest=-30d latest=-1d by Authentication.user, Authentication.src
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
     | fields user, src]
| iplocation src
| table firstSeen, lastSeen, user, app, src, City, Country, count
| sort - firstSeen

| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`

     | fields user, src]

OAuth consent / suspicious app grant Actions on Objectives High
Cloud identity abuse: app gets high-priv scopes, often via consent phishing.
ATT&CKT1528 T1098.001
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication CloudAppEvents
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("Consent to application.","Add OAuth2PermissionGrant.","Add delegated permission grant.")
| project Timestamp, AccountObjectId, AccountDisplayName, ActivityType,
          ActivityObjects, IPAddress, UserAgent
// Entra ID directory audit — user / admin consent to a third-party app.
AuditLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OperationName has "Consent to application"
| where Result =~ "success"
| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Entra ID Consent to Third-Party OAuth Application
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d05
status: experimental
description: |
    User-driven consent to a third-party OAuth application — illicit-
    consent attack precursor. Pair with first-time-seen-app heuristic
    in your SIEM for higher fidelity.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1528/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:

detection:

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-34908", "CVE-2026-34909", "CVE-2026-34910")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

High

Mastra npm Supply Chain Attack: 140+ Packages Backdoored via easy-day-js Typosquat

StepSecurity
2026-06-18 8 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

On June 17, 2026, an attacker compromised the @mastra npm organization and quietly added easy-day-js as a dependency across 140+ packages in the Mastra AI framework ecosystem. easy-day-js is a typosquat of the popular dayjs date library, and its latest version contained an obfuscated postinstall dropper that downloaded and ran a second-stage payload from attacker-controlled servers, then deleted itself to remove any trace. Packages with a combined weekly download count exceeding 1.1 million were exposed. If you installed any @mastra package today, treat your environment as compromised.

IPs23.254.164.92 23.254.164.123

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] easy-day-js malicious setup.cjs written/deleted under node_modules
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Mastra npm Supply Chain Attack: 140+ Packages Backdoored via easy-day-js Typosqu
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Mastra easy-day-js postinstall dropper: node setup.cjs --no-warnings
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] easy-day-js Mastra dropper C2 callout to 23.254.164.92 / .123 · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
easy-day-js Mastra dropper C2 callout to 23.254.164.92 / .123 AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts outbound connections to the two hardcoded easy-day-js second-stage C2 hosts (23.254.164.92, 23.254.164.123, payload at https://23.254.164.92:8000/update/49890878). Any dev workstation or CI agent reaching these IPs indicates a compromised @mastra/easy-day-js install fetching the Sapphire Sleet Node.js RAT. Rationale: Both IPs (23.254.164.92, 23.254.164.123) and the :8000/update/ payload URL are confirmed by JFrog, Socket and Microsoft as dedicated easy-day-js C2 — destination-IP match on confirmed-malicious infra is the highest-fidelity pivot available. Cross-checked against: • https://research.jfrog.com/post/easy-day-js/ • https://socket.dev/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/144-mastra-npm-packages-compromised-via.html

  • Threat-intel platform / sandbox actively probing the IOC IPs for enrichment

  • Honeypot or canary deliberately reaching the C2 to confirm the block

Mastra easy-day-js postinstall dropper: node setup.cjs --no-warnings AIInstallation High
Hunts execution of the easy-day-js postinstall dropper — 'node setup.cjs --no-warnings' — that decodes and runs the obfuscated second-stage loader. The --no-warnings flag is the attacker's deliberate suppression of Node deprecation/experimental warnings during install. Rationale: 'node setup.cjs --no-warnings' is the verbatim postinstall hook from the article and is corroborated by Socket/JFrog; the setup.cjs basename plus the --no-warnings evasion flag together make this near-unique to the dropper. Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised • https://research.jfrog.com/post/easy-day-js/ • https://www.mend.io/blog/mastra-npm-scope-takeover-easy-day-js/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate internal npm package that ships its own setup.cjs install script (rare, and rarely with --no-warnings)
  • Developer manually running a script literally named setup.cjs with --no-warnings for an unrelated project
  • CI pipeline build step invoking a benign setup.cjs
  • Reproduction of the IOC by a researcher on a sanctioned analysis box
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "\\sanctioned-malware-analysis\\")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the parent chain is npm/npx (node running npm-cli.js) and inspect the working directory — node_modules\easy-day-js confirms TP. Capture SHA256 of node and any child process. 2) Contain: kill the node process tree, isolate the host, block the C2 IPs, and remove easy-day-js plus the affected @mastra package versions from the project and npm cache. 3) Hunt: search DeviceNetworkEvents for callouts to 23.254.164.92/.123 from the same host, check for OS login-persistence (Run key / LaunchAgent / systemd) written by node, and pull which @mastra packages/versions are pinned across all repos and CI configs.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName in~ ("node.exe", "node")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "setup.cjs" and ProcessCommandLine has "--no-warnings"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

| where CommandLine has "setup.cjs" and CommandLine has "--no-warnings"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

easy-day-js malicious setup.cjs written/deleted under node_modules AIDelivery High
Hunts the on-disk footprint of the compromised dependency: the 4,572-byte obfuscated setup.cjs dropped into a node_modules\easy-day-js path during install, and its subsequent self-deletion to destroy forensic evidence. Catches the supply-chain artifact even when egress to C2 is already blocked. Rationale: The setup.cjs filename and the easy-day-js package directory are both verbatim article IOCs; the combination of that exact basename within an easy-day-js node_modules path is unique to this campaign and also surfaces the self-delete (FileDeleted) used for anti-forensics. Cross-checked against: • https://research.jfrog.com/post/easy-day-js/ • https://socket.dev/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised • https://www.mend.io/blog/mastra-npm-scope-takeover-easy-day-js/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1070.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher unpacking the easy-day-js tarball on a sanctioned analysis host
  • EDR/AV quarantine or remediation tooling relocating the file (shows as a delete/move)
  • Backup or sync agent copying a node_modules tree that still contains the artifact
  • npm cache extraction of the package during offline triage
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("MsMpEng.exe","MsSense.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm ActionType — a FileCreated/Modified means an active install; a lone FileDeleted with no prior create suggests the dropper already self-removed (pivot to network + process telemetry). Note FileSize ~4572 bytes and the initiating process. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block the C2 IPs, and purge easy-day-js and the affected @mastra versions from the repo, lockfile and npm cache; rotate any secrets exposed to that env. 3) Hunt: enumerate all hosts/CI runners with an easy-day-js folder, correlate to node setup.cjs executions and C2 callouts, and audit package-lock.json across repos for '"easy-day-js": "^1.11.21"'.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

date: 2026/05/02

            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'

            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'
    condition: selection_image and selection_args
falsepositives:
    - DevOps tooling that legitimately uses encoded command for
      cross-shell escaping
level: high

Prevent npm and Python Supply Chain Attacks on Developer Machines with Package Configs

StepSecurity
2026-06-18 6 use cases 2 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

npm and Python supply chain attacks run on developer machines and steal secrets. See how Package Configs audits registry, cooldown, and auth across your fleet

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Miasma/Hades Bun dropper executed via npm/pip lifecycle hook (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) · [LLM] Shai-Hulud/Miasma malicious GitHub Actions workflow file written to .github/workflows
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] TruffleHog secret-scanning spawned by npm/pip during install (Shai-Hulud credential harvest) · [LLM] Developer-runtime exfiltration to webhook.site (Shai-Hulud token drop) · [LLM] Mass npm publish from a developer endpoint (worm self-replication)
Miasma/Hades Bun dropper executed via npm/pip lifecycle hook (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) AIInstallation High
Hunts the Miasma (npm) and Hades (PyPI) worm execution chain, which drops setup_bun.js to fetch the Bun runtime and then runs the obfuscated bun_environment.js credential-stealer during package install. These two filenames are the campaign's distinctive payload artifacts confirmed across Zscaler/Datadog/StepSecurity reporting. Rationale: setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js are the named dropper/payload files of the Miasma/Hades waves, confirmed by Zscaler and Datadog second sources — not present in any legitimate package, so a literal cmdline match is high-fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://www.zscaler.com/blogs/security-research/shai-hulud-campaign-evolution-miasma-hades-and-ai-scanner-evasion • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/the-hades-campaign-pypi-packages
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A developer legitimately scaffolding a Bun-based project that ships its own setup_bun.js helper
  • Internal build tooling that happens to reuse the bun_environment.js name
  • Security researcher detonating a captured sample in a lab endpoint enrolled in production telemetry
  • CI agent image-build steps that pre-warm the Bun runtime
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("\\ci\\","\\buildagent\\") and DeviceName has "build")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full process tree on the host — confirm the parent is npm/node/pip/python (lifecycle hook) and capture the package + version being installed and the SHA256 of the script. 2) Contain: isolate the device, revoke any npm/PyPI/GitHub/cloud publishing tokens that lived on it, and force-rotate credentials referenced in ~/.npmrc, ~/.pypirc and CI secrets. 3) Hunt: search for outbound traffic to webhook.site, new .github/workflows/*.yml writes, and any npm publish activity from this user across the fleet to find lateral republication.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

TruffleHog secret-scanning spawned by npm/pip during install (Shai-Hulud credential harvest) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the Shai-Hulud credential-harvest stage where the malicious postinstall bundle.js downloads and runs TruffleHog to sweep the developer machine for npm/GitHub/AWS/GCP tokens. The discriminator is TruffleHog being launched by a package-manager/runtime process rather than by a CI/security workflow. Rationale: Shai-Hulud's bundle.js is documented (ReversingLabs/Sysdig) to fetch and run TruffleHog to enumerate secrets; the parent-process constraint (node/npm/python) separates worm activity from a security team running TruffleHog manually or in CI. Cross-checked against: • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • DevSecOps team running TruffleHog in a pre-commit hook invoked by node/python tooling
  • Local secret-scan step in a developer's Makefile/npm script before pushing
  • Editor/IDE extension that shells out to TruffleHog through a Node host process
  • Security engineer ad-hoc scanning a repo from a python virtualenv
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "pre-commit" and FolderPath !has "\\.git\\hooks\\"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the TruffleHog run is part of a sanctioned scanning workflow; inspect the parent package/version and whether the binary was freshly downloaded to a temp path. 2) Contain: if unsanctioned, isolate the host and rotate every token TruffleHog could have read (npm/PyPI publish tokens, GH PAT, cloud keys). 3) Hunt: pivot to outbound exfil (webhook.site), new GitHub Actions workflow files, and mass npm publish events tied to the same user/token.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName has "trufflehog" or ProcessCommandLine has "trufflehog")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","bun.exe","python.exe","pip.exe")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessName has "trufflehog" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "trufflehog"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node.exe","npm.cmd","bun.exe","python.exe","pip.exe")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Shai-Hulud/Miasma malicious GitHub Actions workflow file written to .github/workflows AIInstallation High
Hunts on-disk creation of the worm's backdoor CI workflow files (shai-hulud-workflow.yml, shai-hulud.yaml, discussion.yaml) under a repo's .github/workflows directory. These trigger on push and exfiltrate workflow-scoped secrets, and the filenames are confirmed IOCs across multiple vendor write-ups. Rationale: The filenames shai-hulud-workflow.yml / shai-hulud.yaml / discussion.yaml under .github/workflows are documented worm persistence/exfil IOCs (ReversingLabs, Zscaler); pinning to that path keeps the match precise. Cross-checked against: • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm • https://www.zscaler.com/blogs/security-research/shai-hulud-campaign-evolution-miasma-hades-and-ai-scanner-evasion
ATT&CKT1546 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate repo that genuinely names a workflow discussion.yaml (e.g. GitHub Discussions automation)
  • Internal scaffolding tool that templates many workflow files at once
  • Git checkout/clone of a quarantined sample repo onto an analyst box
  • Security test repo intentionally seeded with the IOC for tabletop exercises
Suppression filter
| where not (FileName =~ "discussion.yaml" and InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("git.exe","Code.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the written YAML and confirm it triggers on push and posts secrets to an external URL; identify the repo and the process that wrote it. 2) Contain: delete the workflow, isolate the host, and invalidate all GitHub Actions/repo secrets and any PAT on the device; check the remote repo for the same file pushed upstream. 3) Hunt: enumerate every repo on the host and across the org for these filenames, and pivot to webhook.site exfil and Bun dropper execution on the same machine.

Developer-runtime exfiltration to webhook.site (Shai-Hulud token drop) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts outbound connections to webhook.site initiated by node/bun/python/npm/pip — the secret-exfiltration channel used by Shai-Hulud (confirmed endpoint hxxps://webhook.site/bb8ca5f6-4175-45d2-b042-fc9ebb8170b7). Scoping to package-runtime processes separates worm exfil from humans using webhook.site for legitimate webhook testing. Rationale: webhook.site (and the specific path /bb8ca5f6-4175-45d2-b042-fc9ebb8170b7) is the documented Shai-Hulud exfil sink; constraining the initiator to package runtimes turns a noisy domain into a high-signal supply-chain exfil indicator. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm
ATT&CKT1567 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer using webhook.site to debug a real webhook integration from a Node/Python test script
  • QA harness posting test events to a webhook.site bin
  • Automated integration tests that mock callbacks via webhook.site
  • Sentinel DNS-only view firing on webhook.site lookups with no malicious payload (no process context available)
Suppression filter
DeviceNetworkEvents | where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "test" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "webhook-test"
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the initiating process/package and capture the POST body or DNS frequency; a single token-shaped POST right after install is malicious, repeated test traffic is likely benign. 2) Contain: isolate the host and rotate npm/PyPI/GitHub/cloud credentials that were on disk; block the webhook.site bin at the proxy. 3) Hunt: correlate with setup_bun.js/bun_environment.js execution and new .github/workflows files on the same host, and check other hosts beaconing the same webhook.site path.

| where RemoteUrl has "webhook.site"

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
ImDnsActivity

| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DnsQuery
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Mass npm publish from a developer endpoint (worm self-replication) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the worm's self-replication step: after stealing a publishing token it republishes infected versions of every package the token can publish (the June 3 wave pushed 286 versions across 57 packages in under two hours). Flags a developer host issuing an abnormal burst of npm publish commands in a short window. Rationale: The article states the worm 'republishes infected versions of every package that token can publish'; a per-host burst of npm publish executions is the on-endpoint signature of that self-replication, threshold-derived from the reported 286-versions-in-2h rate. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/miasma-worm-hits-73-microsoft-github.html • https://www.securityweek.com/over-100-npm-pypi-packages-hit-in-new-shai-hulud-supply-chain-attacks/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1072
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Maintainer doing a legitimate monorepo batch release via lerna/changesets/nx that publishes many packages at once
  • CI release runner mistakenly enrolled in endpoint telemetry firing a multi-package publish
  • First-time bulk publish when migrating an org's packages to a new scope
  • An engineer re-publishing several patch versions during a hotfix sprint
Suppression filter
| where SampleCmd !has "lerna" and SampleCmd !has "changeset" and SampleCmd !has "nx release"
Response runbook
1) Triage: review the published package names/versions and whether they match the host owner's normal maintainership; check timing against any sanctioned release. 2) Contain: immediately revoke the npm publishing token, enable 2FA/publish protection on affected packages, isolate the host, and request npm unpublish/deprecation of the suspect versions. 3) Hunt: pivot to TruffleHog execution, webhook.site exfil and Bun dropper artifacts on the same host, and scan downstream consumers for installs of the freshly published versions.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "npm" and ProcessCommandLine has "publish"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| summarize PublishCount = count(), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp), SampleCmd = any(ProcessCommandLine) by DeviceName, AccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1h)
| where PublishCount >= 5   // worm pushed 286 versions across 57 packages in <2h; human maintainers rarely exceed a few publishes/hour
| order by LastSeen desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "npm" and TargetProcessCommandLine has "publish"
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| summarize PublishCount = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), SampleCmd = any(TargetProcessCommandLine) by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| where PublishCount >= 5   // burst threshold; tune to your release cadence
| order by LastSeen desc

| CommandLine=/npm.*publish/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName], function=count(as=PublishCount))
| PublishCount >= 5

| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Critical

400+ AUR Packages Hijacked: What the “Atomic Arch” Campaign Means for Supply-Chain Security

StepSecurity
2026-06-18 8 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

On June 11th 2026, security researchers and the Arch Linux community disclosed a large-scale supply-chain attack against the Arch User Repository (AUR). Attackers hijacked more than 400 community packages and turned them into a malware delivery network. While the immediate blast radius is limited to Arch Linux systems, the campaign is a textbook example of how modern attackers compromise developers and CI infrastructure by abusing trust in open-source ecosystems.

ATT&CKT1190 T1539 T1555.003
Domainstemp.sh github.com/fardewoak/nodejs-argo

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] AUR build pulls malicious npm/Bun dependency (atomic-lockfile / js-digest / lockfile-js)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Atomic Arch payload execution: JS runtime spawning shell/network tooling under AUR build (or known payload hash) · [LLM] Atomic Arch persistence: systemd unit, cron or shell-rc written by AUR build / JS runtime
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Atomic Arch C2/exfil: build-spawned egress to temp.sh and github.com/fardewoak/nodejs-argo · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Atomic Arch infostealer: build-spawned process harvesting SSH keys, dev tokens and browser/Electron sessions · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
AUR build pulls malicious npm/Bun dependency (atomic-lockfile / js-digest / lockfile-js) AIDelivery High
Hunts the Atomic Arch supply-chain delivery: an Arch package build (makepkg/yay/paru/pacman) invoking npm/bun/pnpm to install the campaign's rogue dependencies atomic-lockfile, js-digest or lockfile-js. These package names are the single highest-fidelity IOC of the campaign and are confirmed across Sonatype and The Hacker News. Rationale: Anchored on the three confirmed rogue package names (atomic-lockfile/js-digest/lockfile-js) cross-checked against Sonatype (Sonatype-2026-003775) and The Hacker News; npm names are lowercase-only so no case-folding needed and FPs are near-zero outside the campaign. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sonatype.com/blog/atomic-arch-npm-campaign-adds-malicious-dependency • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/over-400-arch-linux-aur-packages.html • https://ioctl.fail/preliminary-analysis-of-aur-malware/
ATT&CKT1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A security researcher or detection engineer intentionally pulling the IOC packages into a sandbox for analysis
  • An internal mirror/quarantine job that downloads flagged packages for scanning (trivy/grype/snyk) before blocking
  • A developer pasting a remediation command containing the package name into a terminal to verify it is NOT installed
  • Threat-intel enrichment tooling echoing the package name in a build log
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("trivy","grype","snyk","clamscan") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "npm audit"
Response runbook
1) Triage: Confirm the host is Arch/Arch-derivative or WSL2-Arch and pull the full process tree — identify the parent PKGBUILD/AUR package name and the user context (root vs user). Capture install timestamp vs AUR upgrade history since early June 2026. 2) Contain: Isolate the host from the network; the build host must be treated as fully compromised, not cleaned. Block egress to temp.sh. 3) Hunt: Rotate ALL credentials reachable from the host (SSH keys, GitHub/npm tokens, cloud keys, Vault tokens), pivot to the credential-access and C2 UCs on the same DeviceId, and rebuild from trusted media.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("npm","bun","pnpm","yarn","node","npx")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("atomic-lockfile","js-digest","lockfile-js")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          Parent = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DvcOs has "Linux" or isempty(DvcOs)
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("npm","bun","pnpm","yarn","node","npx")
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("atomic-lockfile","js-digest","lockfile-js")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          Parent = ActingProcessName, ParentCmd = ActingProcessCommandLine,
          Child = TargetProcessName, ChildCmd = TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects an Arch package build installing the Atomic Arch rogue npm/Bun packages atomic-lockfile, js-digest or lockfile-js.
references:

Atomic Arch payload execution: JS runtime spawning shell/network tooling under AUR build (or known payload hash) AIInstallation High
Hunts the post-install execution branch of Atomic Arch: a Node/npm/Bun process spawned inside a makepkg/pacman build chain that then launches shells or network/encoding utilities (curl, wget, nc, openssl, base64), or any process matching the confirmed Rust payload SHA-256. Captures the stealer/eBPF dropper executing as root during package install. Rationale: Pairs the campaign's runtime chain (makepkg/pacman -> JS runtime -> shell/egress tooling) with the multi-source-confirmed payload SHA-256 6144d433...c98b, giving an alerting-grade hash clause plus a behavioural net for renamed payloads. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/over-400-arch-linux-aur-packages.html • https://ioctl.fail/preliminary-analysis-of-aur-malware/ • https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-aur-supply-chain-ebpf-rootkit-20260614-csa/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate npm postinstall scripts of native modules (node-gyp / node-pre-gyp / prebuild) spawning sh and downloading prebuilt binaries
  • A developer manually running 'npm install' on a project whose lifecycle scripts call curl/openssl
  • CI bootstrap steps that fetch artefacts via curl invoked from a Node toolchain
  • Electron app build steps invoking base64/openssl for code signing
Suppression filter
| where not (ChildCmd has_any ("node-gyp","node-pre-gyp","prebuild-install","registry.npmjs.org"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: Inspect the grandparent (makepkg/pacman) and the child command line — a curl/nc/openssl invocation with an external host or piped to sh is malicious; node-gyp fetching from registry.npmjs.org / github releases is benign. Confirm the SHA-256 against 6144d433...c98b. 2) Contain: Isolate the host and kill the process tree; preserve /tmp and the user's npm cache for forensics. 3) Hunt: Pivot to the credential-access and C2 UCs on the same DeviceId and check for systemd/cron persistence and eBPF objects.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","bun","pnpm")
        and FileName in~ ("sh","bash","curl","wget","nc","ncat","ssh","base64","openssl","gpg")
        and InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("makepkg","fakeroot","pacman","yay","paru","bash","sh"))
    or SHA256 =~ "6144d433f8a0316869877b5f834c801251bbb936e5f1577c5680878c7443c98b"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessIntegrityLevel,
          GrandParent = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          Parent = InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          Child = FileName, ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where (ActingProcessName has_any ("node","npm","npx","bun","pnpm")
        and TargetProcessName has_any ("sh","bash","curl","wget","nc","ncat","ssh","base64","openssl","gpg")
        and ParentProcessName has_any ("makepkg","fakeroot","pacman","yay","paru"))
    or Hash =~ "6144d433f8a0316869877b5f834c801251bbb936e5f1577c5680878c7443c98b"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          GrandParent = ParentProcessName, Parent = ActingProcessName,
          Child = TargetProcessName, ChildCmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Atomic Arch infostealer: build-spawned process harvesting SSH keys, dev tokens and browser/Electron sessions AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the Rust stealer's credential-collection stage: a Node/npm/Bun process (or a child of makepkg/pacman) reading or copying high-value developer secrets — SSH private keys & known_hosts, ~/.npmrc, ~/.docker/config.json, HashiCorp ~/.vault-token, GitHub CLI hosts.yml, and Chromium/Electron cookie & Login Data stores for Chrome/Edge/Brave/Slack/Discord/Teams/Telegram. Rationale: Targets the exact secret stores Sonatype/THN list as Atomic Arch loot (SSH keys, ~/.npmrc, Docker/Vault tokens, Chromium & Electron cookie DBs) and ties access to the JS-runtime/AUR-build initiator so it reads as theft, not normal app use. Datadog left empty: auditd file-read capture needs per-path watch rules and Datadog's standard Linux schema exposes no reliable file.path facet. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sonatype.com/blog/atomic-arch-npm-campaign-adds-malicious-dependency • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/over-400-arch-linux-aur-packages.html • https://ioctl.fail/preliminary-analysis-of-aur-malware/
ATT&CKT1555.003 T1552.004 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate Node CLI (e.g. an SSH/git wrapper, vault-cli helper, or gh extension) reading ~/.ssh or ~/.config/gh/hosts.yml during normal use
  • Electron apps (Slack/Discord) reading their own cookie DB on launch when the initiator is misattributed to node
  • Developer tooling reading ~/.npmrc for auth during a normal npm install

Response runbook

Atomic Arch persistence: systemd unit, cron or shell-rc written by AUR build / JS runtime AIInstallation Medium
Hunts persistence dropped by the hijacked package: a Node/npm/Bun process (or makepkg-spawned shell) creating a systemd .service unit, a cron entry, an /etc/profile.d script, or modifying shell rc files (.bashrc/.zshrc/.bash_profile). Package managers legitimately ship units to /usr/lib/systemd, so this anchors on the malicious JS-runtime/build initiator and excludes that path. Rationale: Maps the article's explicit guidance to 'monitor for creation of suspicious systemd units' to concrete autostart paths, and scopes the writer to the JS runtime/AUR build so it excludes the normal pacman -> /usr/lib/systemd unit drops that would otherwise flood the rule. Datadog empty: relies on auditd file-watch coverage not in Datadog's standard Linux facet set. Cross-checked against: • https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-aur-supply-chain-ebpf-rootkit-20260614-csa/ • https://www.sonatype.com/blog/atomic-arch-npm-campaign-adds-malicious-dependency
ATT&CKT1543.002 T1053.003 T1546.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate npm global package postinstall that registers a user-level systemd service (e.g. a self-hosted runner or dev daemon)
  • nvm/asdf or a dotfiles manager appending to ~/.bashrc/.zshrc during a normal install
  • A Node-based devtool writing an /etc/profile.d helper for PATH setup
  • Cron entries created by a legitimate Node scheduler package
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("ansible","puppet","chef-client","systemctl","nvm","asdf")
Response runbook
1) Triage: Open the created unit/cron/rc content — a systemd ExecStart or cron line invoking a binary from /tmp, a hidden dir, or curl|sh is malicious; a named, signed dev daemon is likely benign. Correlate the write time with an AUR package install. 2) Contain: Stop and disable the unit/cron entry, isolate the host, and snapshot the file for IR before removal. 3) Hunt: Search for the referenced payload binary's SHA-256 (6144d433...c98b), check for eBPF objects, and sweep other Arch hosts for the same unit name.
DeviceFileEvents

| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")

    or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node","npm","bun","makepkg")
| where (FolderPath has_any ("/etc/systemd/system/","/.config/systemd/user/") and FileName endswith ".service")
    or FolderPath has_any ("/etc/cron.d/","/var/spool/cron/","/etc/cron.daily/","/etc/profile.d/")
    or FileName in~ (".bashrc",".bash_profile",".zshrc",".profile",".bash_logout")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, ActionType,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc

Atomic Arch C2/exfil: build-spawned egress to temp.sh and github.com/fardewoak/nodejs-argo AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the campaign's outbound stage: a Node/npm/Bun/curl/git process launched from an AUR build reaching the exfil/staging endpoints temp.sh (file-sharing exfil) or the fardewoak/nodejs-argo GitHub repo (Cloudflare argo tunnel C2 tooling). Surfaces credential exfil and second-stage C2 retrieval during/after package install. Rationale: Uses the article's two extracted network IOCs (temp.sh exfil host, github.com/fardewoak/nodejs-argo argo-tunnel C2 repo) and scopes the initiator to the AUR build / JS-runtime chain so generic temp.sh usage isn't flagged. github path only surfaces in HTTP-inspected/proxy logs; the DNS/auditd-cmdline anchors (temp.sh, fardewoak, nodejs-argo) execute as-is. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/400-aur-packages-hijacked-atomic-arch-campaign • https://ioctl.fail/preliminary-analysis-of-aur-malware/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1567.002
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A developer legitimately using temp.sh as a quick file-share from a terminal (curl) unrelated to the campaign
  • A CI job that git-clones an unrelated GitHub repo where 'argo' appears in the URL
  • Proxy/SSL-inspection mislabeling the RemoteUrl host
  • Browser-driven downloads from temp.sh (excluded by the process filter)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("firefox","chrome","chromium","thunderbird")
Response runbook

status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:

    product: windows

        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'

            - '\Login Data'

   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"

            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT

Miasma and Hades Are Spreading Now: Detect Them on Developer Machines with Suspicious Files

StepSecurity
2026-06-18 9 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Miasma and Hades worms are spreading across npm and PyPI, running on import and project open. See how Dev Machine Guard's Suspicious Files detects them.

ATT&CKT1027 T1195.002
SHA256dc48b09b2a5954f7… e1342a80d4b5e83d… c539766062555d47… 7069e28a5806db4a… b86c5ae9e95bd841…

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Miasma and Hades Are Spreading Now: Detect Them on Developer Machines with Suspi
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Miasma/Hades auto-exec editor & AI-tool config files dropped in project tree · [LLM] Phantom Gyp: malicious binding.gyp executing during npm install · [LLM] Hades PyPI startup hook: malicious -setup.pth dropped in site-packages · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Hades on-import payload: Python process spawning Bun JavaScript runtime
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Worm propagation: burst of npm/PyPI publishes from a developer machine
Miasma/Hades auto-exec editor & AI-tool config files dropped in project tree AIInstallation Medium
Hunts for the StepSecurity-named 'Suspicious Files' that the Miasma/Hades worm plants into a repository so code runs the moment a developer opens the folder or starts an AI coding session — .claude/setup.mjs, .cursor/rules/setup.mdc, .gemini/settings.json, .vscode/tasks.json (runOn:folderOpen), .vscode/setup.mjs, .github/setup.js. Also matches the campaign's known payload SHA256s. These artifacts never appear in package.json or a lockfile diff, so SCA/registry gateways miss them. Rationale: Uses the exact file/path matrix StepSecurity published (.claude/setup.mjs, .cursor/rules/setup.mdc, .gemini/settings.json, .vscode/tasks.json, .vscode/setup.mjs, .github/setup.js) plus the campaign's five payload SHA256s, cross-checked against Socket.dev and JFrog write-ups of the same Miasma/Hades wave. Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/shai-hulud-descends-to-hades-miasma-pypi-wave • https://research.jfrog.com/post/shai-hulud-miasma-redhat-cloud-services/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/hades-pypi-attack-19-packages-poisoned.html
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately scaffolds a .vscode/tasks.json build task or .claude/settings.json team config and commits it
  • Project templates / cookiecutters that ship a .github or .vscode folder
  • A teammate cloning a known-good internal repo that intentionally uses .cursor/rules
  • VS Code or Cursor itself writing settings.json during normal use
  • Dotfile-manager tools syncing editor configs across machines
Suppression filter
| where not (FileName in~ ("settings.json","tasks.json") and InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("Code.exe","cursor.exe","devenv.exe","git.exe") and FolderPath !has "node_modules")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Open the flagged file and read it — legit tasks.json/settings.json contain JSON config; malicious variants contain a setup.mjs/setup.js loader, an obfuscated one-liner, or a 'runOn: folderOpen' task that shells out (node, curl, bun). Check git blame / commit author for an unexpected or recently re-compromised contributor. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the developer host, revoke that user's npm and PyPI publish tokens (~/.npmrc, ~/.pypirc) and any GitHub PAT, and quarantine the repo from CI. 3) HUNT: Pivot to UC 'Hades Bun runtime' and 'mass publish' detections on the same host; search the org's other repos for the same file paths/hashes; review what packages the leaked tokens could publish.

| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where (FileName in~ ("setup.mjs","setup.js","setup.mdc","tasks.json","settings.json") and FolderPath has_any (".claude",".cursor",".gemini",".vscode",".github"))
    or SHA256 in ("dc48b09b2a5954f7ff79ab8a2fd80202bd3b59c08c7cdbc6025aa923cb4c0efe","e1342a80d4b5e83d2c7c22e1e0aaa95f2d88e3dbf0d853a4994b180c93a4b17d","c539766062555d47716f8432e73adbe3a0c0c954a0b6c4005017a668975e275c","7069e28a5806db4ab0273639667d203f5e31b401d403af7e36d9f360c1f6d655","b86c5ae9e95bd841a595440faa3eb6317441e746f241ae8fd641ab59ed1d1966")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
