Phantom Gyp: malicious binding.gyp executing during npm install AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the Miasma 'Phantom Gyp' technique — a ~157-byte binding.gyp dropped into a package so node-gyp executes attacker code during the native build step, bypassing preinstall/postinstall script controls. Matches binding.gyp written by npm/node tooling that is abnormally small or matches a known campaign hash. Rationale: Anchors on the article's concrete 'Phantom Gyp' detail — a 157-byte binding.gyp that fires during the native build — plus the campaign SHA256 set; Snyk independently documents the same self-propagating binding.gyp npm worm. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/node-gyp-supply-chain-compromise-self-propagating-npm-worm-binding-gyp/ • https://research.jfrog.com/post/shai-hulud-miasma-redhat-cloud-services/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate native npm module ships a genuinely tiny binding.gyp stub
  • Developer hand-authoring a minimal binding.gyp while prototyping a node addon
  • node-gyp regenerating binding.gyp under node_modules during a clean install of a trusted dependency
  • Monorepo build tooling templating placeholder gyp files
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has "node_modules" and FileSize between (100 .. 511) and SHA256 !in ("dc48b09b2a5954f7ff79ab8a2fd80202bd3b59c08c7cdbc6025aa923cb4c0efe","e1342a80d4b5e83d2c7c22e1e0aaa95f2d88e3dbf0d853a4994b180c93a4b17d","c539766062555d47716f8432e73adbe3a0c0c954a0b6c4005017a668975e275c","7069e28a5806db4ab0273639667d203f5e31b401d403af7e36d9f360c1f6d655","b86c5ae9e95bd841a595440faa3eb6317441e746f241ae8fd641ab59ed1d1966"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: cat the binding.gyp — a benign one declares 'targets'/'sources'; the Phantom Gyp variant abuses a gyp 'action'/'rule' to invoke node/curl/bun or contains an inline command. Identify the owning package and whether it was just installed/updated. 2) CONTAIN: Kill any node-gyp/node child it spawned, isolate the host, and revoke npm/PyPI tokens reachable from it. 3) HUNT: Correlate with UC 'Hades Bun runtime' and 'mass publish' on the same host within the install window; check which package version pulled it in and pull that version from your registry/cache.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName =~ "binding.gyp"
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FileSize < 512    // Phantom Gyp payload is ~157 bytes; real native-module configs are far larger
    or SHA256 in ("dc48b09b2a5954f7ff79ab8a2fd80202bd3b59c08c7cdbc6025aa923cb4c0efe","e1342a80d4b5e83d2c7c22e1e0aaa95f2d88e3dbf0d853a4994b180c93a4b17d","c539766062555d47716f8432e73adbe3a0c0c954a0b6c4005017a668975e275c","7069e28a5806db4ab0273639667d203f5e31b401d403af7e36d9f360c1f6d655","b86c5ae9e95bd841a595440faa3eb6317441e746f241ae8fd641ab59ed1d1966")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath, FileSize, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "binding.gyp"
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFileSize < 512
    or TargetFileSHA256 in ("dc48b09b2a5954f7ff79ab8a2fd80202bd3b59c08c7cdbc6025aa923cb4c0efe","e1342a80d4b5e83d2c7c22e1e0aaa95f2d88e3dbf0d853a4994b180c93a4b17d","c539766062555d47716f8432e73adbe3a0c0c954a0b6c4005017a668975e275c","7069e28a5806db4ab0273639667d203f5e31b401d403af7e36d9f360c1f6d655","b86c5ae9e95bd841a595440faa3eb6317441e746f241ae8fd641ab59ed1d1966")

| order by TimeGenerated desc

Hades on-import payload: Python process spawning Bun JavaScript runtime AICommand & Control High
Hunts the Hades PyPI execution chain — a poisoned package's import/startup hook downloads a standalone, signed Bun runtime and runs an obfuscated JavaScript stealer. A python.exe/pip process spawning bun is highly abnormal in an enterprise and is the cleanest behavioural signal for the memory-resident stealer (no malware binary to fingerprint). Rationale: Built on the article's core Hades behaviour — import-time download and execution of a standalone Bun runtime — corroborated by The Hacker News and Socket.dev (Bun stealer fetched at Python startup); python-spawns-bun is rare enough to alert. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/hades-pypi-attack-19-packages-poisoned.html • https://socket.dev/blog/shai-hulud-descends-to-hades-miasma-pypi-wave • https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/hades-campaign-pypi-shai-hulud
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1059.006 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A developer who legitimately uses Bun and invokes it from a Python build/orchestration script
  • Polyglot monorepo tooling where a Python task runner shells out to bun for a JS sub-build
  • CI agent images that pre-warm both Python and Bun toolchains
  • A data-science notebook subprocess launching bun for a frontend asset step
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("\\build\\","frontend","vite","esbuild") and DeviceName has_any ("build","ci","runner"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect the bun command line and the parent python command line — capture which package's __init__.py/.pth triggered it and what URL the bun payload was fetched from. Pull the bun child's network connections. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, kill the bun process, and immediately rotate every credential reachable from that developer (npm/PyPI tokens, cloud keys, GitHub PAT) since the payload reads secrets from process memory. 3) HUNT: Search org-wide for python-spawns-bun and for the Hades *-setup.pth artifact; identify the offending PyPI package + version and block it in your index/proxy.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName =~ "bun.exe" or FileName =~ "bun" or ProcessVersionInfoProductName has "Bun"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe","pythonw.exe","pip.exe","pip3.exe")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has "bun"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("python.exe","python3","pythonw.exe","pip.exe","pip3")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Hades PyPI startup hook: malicious -setup.pth dropped in site-packages AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the Hades persistence/trigger artifact independent vendors confirmed — a '*-setup.pth' file planted in Python site-packages/dist-packages that executes at interpreter startup to fetch the Bun runtime. .pth files run any line beginning with 'import', so a campaign-named setup.pth is an on-startup code-execution hook that SCA scanners never inspect. Rationale: Adds the '*-setup.pth' startup-hook artifact that Socket.dev/JFrog/The Hacker News independently tied to the Hades wave (the article only names __init__.py); .pth-in-site-packages with a campaign naming convention is a high-fidelity, low-FP file signal. Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/shai-hulud-descends-to-hades-miasma-pypi-wave • https://thecybersecguru.com/news/hades-pypi-malware-miasma-supply-chain-attack/ • https://research.jfrog.com/post/shai-hulud-miasma-redhat-cloud-services/
ATT&CKT1546 T1195.002 T1059.006
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate package whose distribution genuinely ships a '<name>-setup.pth' (rare but possible for editable installs)
  • setuptools/pip writing __editable__.*.pth or distutils-precedence.pth during normal installs
  • Virtualenv creation copying a base-environment .pth into a new site-packages
  • In-house package using a .pth for namespace path configuration
Suppression filter
| where FileName !in~ ("distutils-precedence.pth","__editable__.pth") and not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("pip.exe","pip3.exe","python.exe") and FileName startswith "__editable__")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Read the .pth file — a benign one contains only directory paths; the Hades variant has an 'import ...' one-liner that decodes/execs and reaches out for Bun. Note the package name embedded in the filename. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, delete/quarantine the .pth, and rotate npm/PyPI/cloud tokens for that developer. 3) HUNT: Pivot to UC 'python spawns Bun' on the same host around startup; query DeviceTvmSoftwareInventory / pip freeze across the fleet for the offending package and block the version at your PyPI proxy.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FileName endswith "-setup.pth"
| where FolderPath has_any ("site-packages","dist-packages")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFileName endswith "-setup.pth"
| where TargetFilePath has_any ("site-packages","dist-packages")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Worm propagation: burst of npm/PyPI publishes from a developer machine AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the self-replication stage — once Miasma/Hades steals npm/PyPI publishing tokens it republishes infected versions of every package the token can publish. A developer endpoint emitting many 'npm publish' or 'twine upload' invocations in a tight window is automated mass-republish behaviour, not human activity. Thresholded so a normal one-off release won't fire. Rationale: Targets the worm's defining self-replication behaviour described in the article (steal publish tokens, republish every package the token allows) as a publish-burst threshold; cross-checked against JFrog/Socket reporting that the same actor mass-republished across npm and PyPI. Cross-checked against: • https://research.jfrog.com/post/shai-hulud-miasma-redhat-cloud-services/ • https://socket.dev/blog/shai-hulud-descends-to-hades-miasma-pypi-wave • https://phoenix.security/miasma-azure-hades-pypi-supply-chain-worm-2026/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A release engineer running a monorepo/Lerna/changesets publish that pushes many packages at once
  • CI release pipeline executing on a developer's machine instead of a build agent
  • Bulk version bump + publish during a coordinated multi-package release
  • Internal tooling that publishes a fleet of private scoped packages on each merge
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("build","ci","runner","release") or AccountName in~ ("svc-release","build-agent"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Review the SampleCmds — confirm whether this was an intended coordinated release (check change tickets / release calendar) and which registry/scope was targeted; legitimate bulk releases come from a known release owner on a known host. 2) CONTAIN: If unsanctioned, immediately revoke the npm/PyPI publish tokens used, force-unpublish/yank the just-published versions, and isolate the host. 3) HUNT: Pull this host's UC 'python spawns Bun' / 'suspicious config files' hits to find the infection vector; enumerate every package the leaked token can publish and audit those for tampered versions in the last 24h.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "publish" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","npm-cli.js","twine")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| summarize PublishCount = count(), SampleCmds = make_set(ProcessCommandLine, 50), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp) by DeviceName, AccountName, bin(Timestamp, 10m)
| where PublishCount >= 5    // 5+ publishes in 10 min = automated mass republish; a human dev publishes 1-2
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "publish" and ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","npm-cli.js","twine")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| summarize PublishCount = count(), SampleCmds = make_set(ActingProcessCommandLine, 50), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, bin(TimeGenerated, 10m)
| where PublishCount >= 5    // 5+ publishes in 10 min = automated mass republish; a human dev publishes 1-2
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @CommandLine:*publish* (@CommandLine:*npm* OR @CommandLine:*twine*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| regex("publish", field=CommandLine)
| (regex("npm", field=CommandLine) OR regex("twine", field=CommandLine))
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName], function=count(as=PublishCount))
| PublishCount >= 5
CLOUD
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*publish*" AND (Processes.process="*npm*" OR Processes.process="*twine*")) by Processes.dest Processes.user _time span=10m | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | where count >= 5
| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*publish*" AND (Processes.process="*npm*" OR Processes.process="*twine*")) by Processes.dest Processes.user _time span=10m | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | where count >= 5

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["dc48b09b2a5954f7ff79ab8a2fd80202bd3b59c08c7cdbc6025aa923cb4c0efe", "e1342a80d4b5e83d2c7c22e1e0aaa95f2d88e3dbf0d853a4994b180c93a4b17d", "c539766062555d47716f8432e73adbe3a0c0c954a0b6c4005017a668975e275c", "7069e28a5806db4ab0273639667d203f5e31b401d403af7e36d9f360c1f6d655", "b86c5ae9e95bd841a595440faa3eb6317441e746f241ae8fd641ab59ed1d1966"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Killing me gently: Inside Gentlemen’s EDR killer framework

ESET WeLiveSecurity
2026-06-18 3 use cases 1 technique 1 kill-chain phase detected

ESET Research shares the results of a months-long investigation into the suite of EDR killers maintained by the RaaS gang Gentlemen

ATT&CKT1486
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")

Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

    selection_psexec:

            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high

Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")

// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the

SecurityEvent

   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"

   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children

description: |

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High

ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766") or SHA1 in~ ("f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766") or MD5 in~ ("f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766") or SHA1 in~ ("f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766") or MD5 in~ ("f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["f7d335205b8d7b20208fb3ef93ee6dc817905dc3ae0c10a0b164f4e7d07121cd", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-20253")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-20253")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Sapphire Sleet easy-day-js RAT C2 beacon to Hostwinds 23.254.164.92 / 23.254.164.123 AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts outbound connections to the two confirmed Hostwinds C2 hosts used by the Mastra/easy-day-js supply-chain RAT: 23.254.164.92:8000 (first-stage dropper download) and 23.254.164.123:443 (second-stage RAT callback). Connections from node.exe to these IPs during or shortly after an npm install are near-certain compromise. Rationale: Anchored on the two C2 IPs (23.254.164.92:8000, 23.254.164.123:443) confirmed by Microsoft, StepSecurity and JFrog as the dropper-download and RAT-callback hosts; pure IOC match so alerting-tier. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised-using-easy-day-js • https://research.jfrog.com/post/easy-day-js/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1105
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel detonation sandbox or malware-analysis VM deliberately running the sample
  • Security researcher reproducing the supply-chain attack in a lab
  • Honeypot/canary host intentionally executing the malicious package
  • Shared Hostwinds VPS range later re-allocated to a benign tenant (validate IP is still flagged before alerting long-term)
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm which local process opened the socket (expect node.exe) and whether an `npm install`/`npm ci` ran on the host in the prior 5 minutes; pull the parent npm command line and the user context. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the node.exe RAT process tree, and block 23.254.164.92 + 23.254.164.123 at the egress proxy/firewall. 3) Hunt: enumerate node_modules for easy-day-js@1.11.22 and any @mastra package at the malicious versions; rotate any developer npm tokens, browser-stored crypto-wallet seeds and session cookies on the host (RAT inventories 166 wallet extensions).

easy-day-js postinstall dropper spawning node.exe with C2 IP passed as argument AIInstallation High
Hunts the dropper's second-stage launch: setup.cjs spawns node.exe against a temp .js file passing the RAT C2 host '23.254.164.123:443' as a command-line argument. The literal C2 IP in a node command line is a near-zero-FP signal of the easy-day-js dropper. Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim dropper code `child_process.spawn(process.execPath, [file, '23.254.164.123:443'])` — node.exe carrying the C2 IP as argv is an exact-string IOC, hence alerting. Cross-checked against: • https://research.jfrog.com/post/easy-day-js/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware-analysis sandbox detonating the sample
  • Lab reproduction of the attack chain by a researcher
  • Highly unlikely benign coincidence: an internal tool legitimately taking 23.254.164.123 as an argument (validate ownership before suppressing)
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full process tree — expect npm/npx -> node (setup.cjs) -> node (<temp>\<hex>.js 23.254.164.123:443); note the temp script path and the spawning user. 2) Contain: isolate host, terminate the detached node.exe RAT, and quarantine the temp .js (it self-deletes setup.cjs, so grab it fast). 3) Hunt: search the same user/host for easy-day-js in node_modules and outbound traffic to 23.254.164.92/.123; review browser wallet-extension and history access by the RAT.

| where ProcessCommandLine has "23.254.164.123"
| where FileName =~ "node.exe" or InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc

Malicious easy-day-js package installed into node_modules AIDelivery High
Hunts the supply-chain footprint itself: files for the typosquatted easy-day-js package (a malicious dayjs clone) being written into node_modules, including its setup.cjs postinstall dropper. Presence indicates the host pulled the compromised @mastra dependency tree. Rationale: Anchored on the concrete package name `easy-day-js` (the malicious dayjs clone, v1.11.22) named in the article and every corroborating vendor write-up; the path node_modules\easy-day-js is unique to this campaign. Cross-checked against: • https://www.ox.security/blog/easy-day-js-supply-chain-attack-hits-mastra-ai-in-npm/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised-using-easy-day-js

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security team intentionally caching the malicious package for analysis
  • SCA/registry mirror snapshotting the package for blocklisting
  • Researcher cloning the repo to study the dropper
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the installed version is easy-day-js@1.11.22 (1.11.21 is the clean decoy) and identify which @mastra packages pulled it via ^1.11.21 caret resolution. 2) Contain: remove node_modules and lockfile, pin/forbid easy-day-js, and check for outbound 23.254.164.92/.123 traffic and detached node.exe processes proving the postinstall fired. 3) Hunt: sweep all build agents/dev laptops/CI runners that ran npm install since 2026-06-16 07:05 UTC; rotate npm tokens and any secrets exposed during builds.

Node.js writing a random 24-hex-char .js dropper to the OS temp directory AIInstallation Medium
Behavioural fallback that survives C2-IP rotation: detects node.exe writing a randomly-named JavaScript file (crypto.randomBytes(12) -> 24 lowercase hex chars + .js) into the OS temp directory, which is exactly how the easy-day-js postinstall stages its second-stage payload before spawning it detached. Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim dropper line `path.join(os.tmpdir(), crypto.randomBytes(12).toString('hex') + '.js')` — 12 random bytes hex-encode to a 24-char lowercase pattern, a tight behavioural signature independent of the (rotatable) C2 IPs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/over-140-popular-mastra-npm-packages-hit-by-supply-chain-attack • https://research.jfrog.com/post/easy-day-js/
ATT&CKT1105 T1059.007 T1070.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Build tools that hash-name temp scripts (some bundlers/test runners like jest/ts-node cache transforms in temp)
  • Electron/node CLI apps writing scratch scripts to temp
  • Local dev tooling generating temp ESM shims
  • AV/EDR sandbox that itself drops node temp scripts
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("jest","ts-node","webpack","vite","\\AppData\\Local\\Programs\\") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: check whether the temp .js still exists (the dropper self-deletes setup.cjs, often leaving the staged file briefly) and whether the writing node.exe parent is an npm install; capture the file for analysis. 2) Contain: if a detached node.exe spawned from that temp path is beaconing to 23.254.164.123, isolate the host and kill the process tree. 3) Hunt: correlate with easy-day-js in node_modules and any 23.254.164.92/.123 connections; widen to other dev hosts that ran npm install in the same window.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where FolderPath has @"\Temp\"
| where FileName matches regex @"^[0-9a-f]{24}\.js$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, FolderPath, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActingProcessName has "node.exe"
| where TargetFilePath has @"\Temp\"
| where TargetFileName matches regex @"^[0-9a-f]{24}\.js$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, DvcAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/

            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'

            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'

        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`

PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

status: experimental
description: |

    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net

references:

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Critical

A Forgotten Contributor Account Compromised the Entire Mastra npm Package Scope

Snyk
2026-06-16 14 use cases 7 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

A dormant contributor account was used to republish the entire @mastra npm scope, each injected with a single dependency, easy-day-js, that drops a cross-platform cryptocurrency stealer. Here is how the attack worked, how to check exposure, and how to remediate.

npm install pulls malicious easy-day-js dropper (setup.cjs + .pkg marker files) AIInstallation High
Hunts a build/dev host resolving the trojanized easy-day-js dependency: the package landing under node_modules\easy-day-js, its setup.cjs postinstall dropper, and the ~/.pkg_history / ~/.pkg_logs marker files the dropper writes to track installs. Article-specific because these exact filenames and the easy-day-js package path are the carrier injected into the @mastra scope. Rationale: Anchors on the exact carrier filenames named by Snyk and corroborated by Microsoft/StepSecurity: the easy-day-js node_modules path, setup.cjs postinstall dropper, and the .pkg_history/.pkg_logs markers — none of which exist on a clean host. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised-using-easy-day-js • https://socket.dev/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A security researcher detonating the sample in a sandbox/analysis VM
  • Defenders deliberately caching the malicious tarball for IR/forensics
  • A renamed-but-unrelated package legitimately named easy-day-js in an internal registry mirror (rare)
  • Backup/EDR agents re-scanning an already-quarantined node_modules tree
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the host resolved easy-day-js@1.11.21/1.11.22 — check the project lockfile and whether npm install ran on/after 2026-06-17; pull the SHA256 of any setup.cjs found. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill any node-spawned detached process, and treat it as credential+wallet exposure — force-reset npm tokens, cloud creds, and browser-stored secrets used on that host; revoke/rotate any wallet seed phrases. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same DeviceId for outbound connections to 23.254.164.92/.123 and persistence under C:\ProgramData\NodePackages; sweep CI runners and other dev laptops sharing the lockfile.
DeviceFileEvents

| where (FileName in~ ("setup.cjs",".pkg_history",".pkg_logs")) or FolderPath has @"\node_modules\easy-day-js\"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node dropper fetches second stage from Hostwinds raw IP 23.254.164.92:8000 AIDelivery High
Hunts the easy-day-js dropper's second-stage download: an outbound connection to the hardcoded Hostwinds host 23.254.164.92 on port 8000 (https://23.254.164.92:8000/update/49890878), typically initiated by node.exe after TLS verification is disabled. High-fidelity because the IP+port are dedicated attacker infrastructure confirmed by multiple vendors. Rationale: Uses the verbatim dropper URL host/port (23.254.164.92:8000/update/49890878) from the article; Microsoft and StepSecurity independently list this as the stage-2 download endpoint, so a hit is near-certainly the dropper. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised-using-easy-day-js
ATT&CKT1105 T1571
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel/sandbox detonation of the sample making the same fetch
  • A blue-team connectivity test confirming the IOC is reachable/blocked
  • Shared NAT egress where another tool coincidentally targets the same /24 (verify exact IP+port)
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the InitiatingProcess — a node.exe/npm child reaching this IP confirms dropper execution; capture the host, user, and the resolved second-stage file in %TEMP%. 2) Contain: block 23.254.164.92 at the egress firewall, isolate the host, and treat as credential+wallet compromise (rotate dev/cloud/npm tokens, browser secrets). 3) Hunt: from the same host pivot to 23.254.164.123 beacons and C:\ProgramData\NodePackages persistence; block the full 23.254.164.0/24 Hostwinds range pending review.

Cross-platform stealer RAT C2 beacon to 23.254.164.123 AICommand & Control High
Hunts the second-stage crypto-stealer/RAT beaconing to its C2 at 23.254.164.123 roughly every 10 minutes over a self-signed wolfSSL channel. Aggregates repeat connections per host to surface the sustained beacon cadence rather than a single hit, distinguishing it from the one-shot dropper download. Rationale: Anchors on the article's RAT C2 IP (23.254.164.123) and the ~10-minute beacon described; the count>=3 aggregation captures the sustained cadence and filters single stray probes. No Sigma emitted because the detection is a threshold/aggregation, not a single-event shape. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.mend.io/blog/mastra-npm-scope-takeover-easy-day-js/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1573
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Sandbox/IR detonation of the stealer producing repeat beacons
  • Security tooling actively probing the IOC for reachability
  • Another host in the same /24 being contacted for unrelated reasons (pin to exact IP)
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm beacon cadence and the originating process; a ~10-min periodic connection from a non-browser process is the RAT. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block 23.254.164.123 at egress, and snapshot memory before reimage; treat all secrets entered on the host as compromised. 3) Hunt: enumerate persistence (C:\ProgramData\NodePackages on Windows, ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.nvm.protocal.plist on macOS, ~/.config/systemd/user/nvmconf.service on Linux) and check other hosts beaconing to the same /24.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP == "23.254.164.123"
| summarize ConnCount=count(), BeaconHours=dcount(bin(Timestamp, 1h)),
            FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp),
            Ports=make_set(RemotePort, 5), Proc=any(InitiatingProcessFileName)
            by DeviceName, RemoteIP
| where ConnCount >= 3   // >=3 hits = repeat beacon, not a stray probe
| order by ConnCount desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr == "23.254.164.123"
| summarize ConnCount=count(), BeaconHours=dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)),
            FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated),
            Ports=make_set(DstPortNumber, 5) by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr
| where ConnCount >= 3   // >=3 hits = repeat beacon, not a stray probe
| order by ConnCount desc

| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, RemoteAddressIP4, RemotePort], function=count())
| count >= 3

easy-day-js stealer persistence masquerading as Node tooling (NodePackages / LaunchAgent / systemd) AIInstallation High
Hunts the cross-platform persistence the stealer installs while disguised as Node tooling: Windows staging under C:\ProgramData\NodePackages, macOS LaunchAgent com.nvm.protocal.plist, and Linux systemd user service nvmconf.service. Article-specific because these exact persistence names are unique to this campaign. Rationale: Uses the three campaign-unique persistence names from the article (C:\ProgramData\NodePackages, com.nvm.protocal.plist, nvmconf.service) — the misspelled 'protocal' and 'nvmconf' are distinctive enough that a hit is effectively a true positive. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.kodemsecurity.com/resources/mastra-npm-packages-compromised-easy-day-js-supply-chain-attack-iocs-and-response-runbook
ATT&CKT1543.001 T1543.002 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate internal tool coincidentally writing to C:\ProgramData\NodePackages (verify against the exact plist/service names)
  • Restored backup re-creating a previously-quarantined persistence file
  • IR responder copying the artifact for analysis
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the persistence file content points to a NodePackages-staged payload or beacon; capture the writing process. 2) Contain: remove the LaunchAgent/systemd unit/Run entry, kill the persisted process, isolate the host, and rotate all credentials/wallets used there. 3) Hunt: correlate with 23.254.164.92/.123 traffic and the easy-day-js install artifacts on the same and peer hosts; sweep all OS types since persistence is cross-platform.

Mastra easy-day-js second-stage stealer payload by SHA256 AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the obfuscated cross-platform crypto-stealer/RAT second stage by file hash on both write and execution. Hash matching is resilient to IP/infra rotation and is effectively zero-FP; the primary hash (221c45...) is the article-named stage-2 payload corroborated by Microsoft. Rationale: Primary hash 221c45a7... is the article-named second-stage payload and is corroborated by Microsoft Threat Intelligence; the additional campaign hashes are bundled IOCs. Hash matching is zero-FP and survives infrastructure rotation, complementing the IP-based UCs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/17/postinstall-payload-inside-mastra-npm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://socket.dev/blog/mastra-npm-packages-compromised
ATT&CKT1555.003 T1070.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware-analysis/sandbox host where the sample was intentionally executed
  • EDR/AV quarantine copy retaining the hash on disk
  • Threat-intel platform caching the sample for enrichment
Response runbook
1) Triage: any hit is high-confidence malicious — confirm host, user, and whether the hash was written vs executed; the payload self-deletes, so a FileWrite without a later process may indicate cleanup. 2) Contain: isolate the host, capture memory, and treat as full credential+wallet compromise — rotate cloud/npm/dev secrets and assume browser-stored wallet data exfiltrated. 3) Hunt: pivot from the host to 23.254.164.92/.123 traffic and NodePackages/LaunchAgent/systemd persistence; submit the hash to org-wide retro-hunt across EDR.

| order by Timestamp desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic

    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic

PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9") or SHA1 in~ ("221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9") or MD5 in~ ("221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9") or SHA1 in~ ("221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9") or MD5 in~ ("221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "b122a9873bedf145ae2a7fd024b5f309007dbb025149f4dc4ac3f7e4f32a36a4", "c38954e85bf5433e61e7c8f4230336695624ae88b6953afabf7bf817aa91b638", "cdec8b20338beb708b5be8d3d7a3041a35a8b0fb92f9186262f312d55ff82066", "9570f77a5e1511869f4e554e7166df9fde081f2583e293c2569621792ed7d9c9"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - None on a true-positive hash match
level: critical

Multiple JetBrains IDE plugins caught stealing AI keys

Aikido
2026-06-16 8 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

A coordinated campaign of at least 15 JetBrains IDE plugins, published under seven vendor accounts, exfiltrates the AI provider API key you paste into their settings. Category: Vulnerabilities & Threats

ATT&CKT1176 T1190 T1195.002
IPs39.107.60.51

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Malicious JetBrains Marketplace plugin install (15 DeepSeek/CodeGPT clone IDs) · Suspicious browser extension installation
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] JetBrains AI-key stealer HTTP exfil: cleartext POST to /api/software/ path · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] JetBrains IDE plugin AI-key exfil: endpoint egress to C2 39.107.60.51 · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
JetBrains IDE plugin AI-key exfil: endpoint egress to C2 39.107.60.51 AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts any host making an outbound connection to the hardcoded attacker C2 39.107.60.51 used by 15 trojanized JetBrains Marketplace plugins (DeepSeek/CodeGPT clones) to ship stolen OpenAI/DeepSeek/SiliconFlow API keys. The connection is initiated by the JetBrains IDE JVM the instant a user clicks Apply on the plugin settings, so the process context is a Java/JetBrains runtime, not a browser. Rationale: Anchored on the single hardcoded C2 IP 39.107.60.51 (verbatim from the article's network-call code block, defanged 39.107.60[.]51) — a dedicated attacker host with no legitimate use, cross-confirmed by BleepingComputer and The Hacker News. Any connection is dispositive. Cross-checked against: • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-jetbrains-marketplace-plugins-steal-ai-api-keys-from-developers/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/malicious-jetbrains-plugins-steal-ai.html • https://blog.jetbrains.com/platform/2026/06/marketplace-ecosystem-security-update-malicious-ai-plugins/
ATT&CKT1041 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A security researcher/sandbox detonating one of the named plugins to reproduce the IOC
  • Threat-intel or attack-surface scanners (Shodan/Censys) probing the IP from a corporate egress
  • IP reuse: the Alibaba Cloud address later reassigned to a benign tenant (validate WHOIS/first-seen date)
  • A SOAR/enrichment playbook auto-resolving or pinging the IOC and generating an event
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the InitiatingProcess is a JetBrains runtime (idea64.exe/pycharm64.exe/java/jetbrains-runtime) and pull the user; enumerate that user's installed JetBrains plugins against the 15 named IDs. 2) Contain: treat every AI provider API key (OpenAI/DeepSeek/SiliconFlow) entered on that host as compromised — revoke and rotate immediately, uninstall the plugin, and block 39.107.60.51 at egress. 3) Hunt: sweep DeviceNetworkEvents org-wide for the IP, and pivot to UC 'malicious JetBrains plugin install' to find dormant infections that haven't yet phoned home.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP == "39.107.60.51"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, Protocol,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DestinationIP == "39.107.60.51"
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, SourceHostName,
          SourceUserName, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, RequestURL,
          ApplicationProtocol, RequestClientApplication
| order by TimeGenerated desc

JetBrains AI-key stealer HTTP exfil: cleartext POST to /api/software/ path AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the application-layer signature of the theft in web-proxy/network-sensor telemetry: a plaintext HTTP POST to the URI path /api/software/<name> carrying the victim's API key as JSON. This survives C2 IP rotation by keying on the distinctive hardcoded URL path, and catches IDE traffic that egresses through a proxy where EDR socket attribution is weak. Rationale: Uses two verbatim article strings — the URL path /api/software/ from the network-call code block and the C2 IP 39.107.60.51 — over plaintext HTTP (article: 'reached over plain HTTP'). The path keys the detection independently of the IP. Datadog/Falcon left empty: standard FDR network events and the listed Datadog sources don't carry HTTP URI paths, so this needs proxy/web-gateway telemetry. Cross-checked against: • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-jetbrains-marketplace-plugins-steal-ai-api-keys-from-developers/ • https://www.infosecurity-magazine.com/news/fifteen-jetbrains-marketplace/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1041
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An internal/SaaS API that legitimately exposes a '/api/software/' inventory endpoint reached over HTTP
  • Software-asset-management or patch agents POSTing to a vendor '/api/software/' path
  • Web-app vulnerability scanners fuzzing '/api/' routes from a corporate host
  • Proxy replay/cached events double-counting the same flow
Suppression filter
| where DstIpAddr == "39.107.60.51" or Url has "39.107.60.51/api/software/"
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the full request — confirm the host header/destination resolves to 39.107.60.51 and, if PCAP/full-proxy is available, look for the static header X-Api-Key: F48D2AA7CF341F782C1D and a JSON body containing an 'sk-' key. 2) Contain: rotate/revoke the leaked provider key(s), remove the offending JetBrains plugin, and add both the IP and the URI path as egress-block + proxy-deny rules. 3) Hunt: search proxy logs for any other host hitting /api/software/ (catches infections after a C2 IP change) and correlate with the endpoint-egress UC.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType == "HttpConnectionInspected"
| where RemoteUrl has "/api/software/" or RemoteUrl has "39.107.60.51"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImWebSession

| where HttpMethod == "POST"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber,
          Url, HttpMethod, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DvcHostname
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Malicious JetBrains Marketplace plugin install (15 DeepSeek/CodeGPT clone IDs) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts on-disk presence or install of any of the 15 trojanized JetBrains plugins by matching their Marketplace display names under a JetBrains \plugins\ directory. Covers the initial-access/supply-chain step and surfaces dormant infections on hosts that haven't yet entered an API key or phoned home to the C2. Rationale: Anchored on the article's verbatim list of 15 malicious plugin display names installed under JetBrains \plugins\ paths — the supply-chain delivery artifact. Hunting-tier because the 'CodeGPT AI Assistant' clone name overlaps the legitimate CodeGPT plugin, so matches need confirmation (pair with the C2-egress UC). Cross-checked against: • https://blog.jetbrains.com/platform/2026/06/marketplace-ecosystem-security-update-malicious-ai-plugins/ • https://hackread.com/malicious-jetbrains-plugins-steal-deepseek-openai-api-keys/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1528
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • The legitimate 'CodeGPT' plugin (genuine vendor) whose folder name substring overlaps the malicious 'CodeGPT AI Assistant' clone
  • Developers who installed an affected plugin before JetBrains' takedown and have since updated/removed it (stale on-disk artifact)
  • Security team deliberately installing a sample for analysis in a lab VM
  • JetBrains Marketplace cache/temp extraction folders retaining the plugin name after backend disable
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("explorer.exe") and FolderPath !has "\\Marketplace\\cache\\"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the exact plugin ID inside the matched folder's plugin.xml against the 15 known IDs (e.g. org.sm.yms.toolkit, com.my.code.tools, ord.cp.code.ai.kit) — this disambiguates the malicious 'CodeGPT AI Assistant' from the legitimate CodeGPT. 2) Contain: uninstall the plugin, and immediately revoke/rotate any OpenAI/DeepSeek/SiliconFlow API key the developer may have entered (assume compromised even without observed egress). 3) Hunt: pivot every matched host to the C2-egress and HTTP-path UCs for 39.107.60.51 to confirm exfiltration, and query software inventory across the fleet for the remaining plugin IDs.

    "DeepSeek AI Assist","Coding Simple Tool")

          by DeviceName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by LastSeen desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.

description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)

FishMonger’s arsenal upgraded: SprySOCKS for Windows

ESET WeLiveSecurity
2026-06-16 6 use cases 0 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

ESET researchers have discovered SprySOCKS for Windows, FishMonger’s backdoor weaponizing a kernel driver for advanced stealthiness

CVEsCVE-2023-24932

SHA1955BFC3DCC867256… 44DC4A08C5EB0972… AB87B29B6F79487C… 6490B8E4AADE25A3… E7484C24B88A1A24…
